@@24369382 <h> Dear President Trump , please never tweet about soccer again <p> Donald Trump might have broken a FIFA rule with his tweet about the 2026 World Cup bid . ( Getty ) <p> Donald Trump tweeted about soccer on Thursday . And , in a wholly unsurprising development , he had no idea what he is talking about . <p> Specifically , President Trump tweeted about the North American bid to host the 2026 World Cup . And if he would like that bid to succeed , he would be best off never tweeting about it again . <p> Trump effectively threatened political repercussions for countries whose soccer federations do n't vote for the " United Bid " -- the U.S. , Canada and Mexico -- over its only competitor , Morocco . <p> The U.S. has put together a STRONG bid w/ Canada &amp; Mexico for the 2026 World Cup . It would be a shame if countries that we always support were to lobby against the U.S. bid . Why should we be supporting these countries when they do n't support us ( including at the United Nations ) ? <p> And for a few reasons , for the sake of the United Bid , that 's about the worst thing the president could have spent his Thursday evening doing . <h> Trump basically just broke FIFA rules <p> First of all , and probably most importantly , FIFA , global soccer 's governing body , has a rule that explicitly prohibits " any form of political interference " in the sport . And in the past , national soccer federations -- such as , recently , Pakistan 's and Guatemala 's -- have been suspended from international competition for violating the rule . <p> It mostly concerns soccer federations ' bylaws and countries ' laws , and cross-pollination involving the two entities . FIFA wants the federations operating independently . But it has been known to inconsistently and selectively enforce the rule . Trump 's tweet would seemingly be worth a warning at the very least . <p> But the bigger issue here is that , because of FIFA 's rule , the United States government 's " support " of other nations at a political level has nothing to do with who will vote for whom on June 13 . Countries do n't vote ; their soccer federations do . If politicians are influencing those federations , that constitutes government interference , and FIFA can take action . <p> So , in other words , Trump 's tweet was n't a great idea . <h> Trump also called it the " U.S. bid " ... <p> ... And that 's precisely a perception that the United Bid wants to shed . It did n't help itself by revealing a plan where 60 of the 80 games would be held in the U.S. , with 10 each in Mexico and Canada . But in more recent times , it has moved to present itself as more of an equal effort . <p> For example , in March , former U.S. Soccer president Sunil Gulati stepped down as the bid 's chairman . He was replaced at the top by three co-chairs : Canada Soccer president Steven Reed , Mexico Football Federation president Decio de Maria , and new U.S. Soccer president Carlos Cordeiro . More than anything , it was a public relations ploy . And other recent PR efforts have attempted to sway popular perception in similar ways . <p> So Trump 's reference to the North American bid as the " U.S. bid " is not ideal . <p> In fact , Trump associating himself at all with the bid is problematic . Because he himself is one of the reasons the bid leaders want to distance themselves from the idea that this is a U.S.-led venture . <h> Trump is already hurting the bid <p> One of several factors keeping Morocco in this race as a legitimate contender for 2026 is the global perception of the Trump-led U.S. Several reports have indicated that . Gulati did n't explicitly say it , but at a convention in Philadelphia in January , he did say , " This bid is not only about our stadiums and our hotels and all that . It 's about perceptions of America , and it 's a difficult time in the world . " <p> Gulati continued , implicitly referencing some of the Trump administration 's foreign policy decisions : " We ca n't control what happens at the 38th parallel in Korea , we ca n't control what happens with embassies in Tel Aviv , and we ca n't control what happens with climate change accords . " <p> Essentially , the North American bid is asking for votes from 207 people , or groups of people , whose fellow citizens do n't like the U.S. because they do n't like Trump . A recent Gallup poll of people in 134 countries revealed that the worldwide approval rating of U.S. leadership has dipped to 30 percent , the lowest recorded since the poll was first conducted over a decade ago . <p> So while politicians ca n't directly influence the vote , their actions indirectly can . Every FIFA member association not involved in the bidding -- so 207 of 211 -- has one vote . Germany 's vote counts for as much as Guam 's . If a few soccer officials in tiny countries -- perhaps some of which Trump infamously called " shithole countries " -- have personal distastes for Trump 's policies , and back Morocco 's bid as a result , they could sway the vote . <p> In other words , Trump really needs to distance himself from this bid as much as possible -- even if he 's not breaking FIFA rules , and even if he 's not misunderstanding the voting process . So please , President Trump , never tweet about soccer again . 
@@24381609 <h> In the article , the writer expresses hope that heart-to-heart talks between the two leaders will help to close the perception gap and restore strategic trust . <p> Yi Fan <p> BEIJING ( CHINA DAILY/ASIA NEWS NETWORK ) - The world will be watching as Chinese President Xi Jinping welcomes Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi to Wuhan in Central China 's Hubei province on Friday ( April 27 ) for the first " informal " summit between the two countries ' leaders . <p> This somewhat surprise diplomatic initiative comes at an important time for China-India relations , considered one of the most consequential for the 21st century . Yet bilateral relations , at times , have been affected by historical grievances and geostrategic tensions . <p> It will therefore be good for the two countries , as well as the world , if they manage their ties well . <p> For more than 2,000 years , the two ancient civilisations have had a friendly association , which has been recorded in numerous stories and Buddhist relics of exchange . <p> In modern times , the two countries supported each other in their struggles for national independence and , along with Myanmar , initiated Panchasheel , or the Five Principles of Peaceful Coexistence , enriching international relations . <p> Today , both China and India are at a crucial stage of reform and modernisation . There is a lot they can share with each other to achieve their similar goals-the Chinese Dream of national rejuvenation and the vision of " New India " . <p> China is the biggest trading partner of India , and India the largest project contracting market for Chinese companies in South Asia . <p> India 's competitive edge in information technology , software and medicines , and China 's strengths in manufacturing , infrastructure development and emerging industries make the two sides natural partners with great potential for closer economic cooperation . <p> The two countries also share the common interest of tackling global issues , from climate change to cybersecurity . <p> President Xi and Prime Minister Modi developed an early interest in each other 's countries . <p> Xi was an avid reader of Rabindranath Tagore 's poems , and Modi visited China four times to draw inspiration for development as chief minister of Gujarat . <p> As national leaders , the two have met 11 times in four years , with exchange visits to each other 's home towns of Ahmedabad , Gujarat province , and Xi'an , Shaanxi province , in 2014 and 2015 respectively . <p> Such frequent interactions and rare show of friendship underscored the great importance both countries attach to their relations and fostered an effective working relationship between the two leaders . <p> Still , bilateral relations are sometimes rattled by the issue of misperceptions , leading to misreading of intentions and trust deficit . <p> Given the growing economic asymmetry between the two countries - India 's GDP is only one-fifth that of China - some in India tend to see the development of China and India in zerosum terms . <p> They interpret India 's failure to gain accession to the UN Security Council and the Nuclear Suppliers Group , and Beijing 's pursuit of the Belt and Road Initiative with other South Asian countries as China 's steps to gain geostrategic advantage over India and thwart its efforts to become a " leading power " . <p> On its part , China will be watching India 's role in the " Indo-Pacific " strategy initiated by the United States keeping China in mind . <p> In this context , the Wuhan meeting provides a much needed opportunity for strategic communication at the top level . <p> The two leaders are expected to hold in-depth talks in a relaxed setting on issues vital to the long-term development of Sino-Indian ties . <p> Rather than focusing on specific issues , such heart-to-heart talks can seek to close the perception gap and restore strategic trust , and form new consensus in order to put bilateral ties back on the track of steady growth . <p> The two leaders are expected to reaffirm their common vision for peace , stability and development of Asia , which is big enough for the neighbouring countries to develop side by side . <p> The common interests between China and India far outweigh their differences . <p> So they should see each other as friends and partners in building a community of shared future for humankind , rather than foes or rivals destined to be trapped in futile competition . <p> Together , they should reject the zero-sum approach and embrace win-win cooperation , because greater synergy of development strategies , the progress of the Belt and Road Initiative , and third-party cooperation in South Asia will benefit both . <p> As Xi has stressed , the Chinese dragon and the Indian elephant must not fight but dance with each other . If they succeed in doing so in Wuhan and in the future , Asia and the world will be better off for it . <p> The author is a Beijing-based expert on international affairs . @@24382418 <h> As water shortages grow , ' Day Zero ' becomes everyday in India <h> An expanding population , growing demand for water from agriculture and industry , and poor management of water supplies have sent India 's groundwater to ever lower levels . <p> Nearly 163 million people among India 's population of 1.3 billion lack access to clean water close to their home , according to a 2018 WaterAid report . Image : Balaram Mahalder , CC BY-SA 3.0 , via Wikimedia Commons <p> Nearly 163 million people among India 's population of 1.3 billion - or more than one in 10 - lack access to clean water close to their home , according to a 2018 WaterAid report . <p> That is the most of any country in the world , according to the UK-based charity , which aims to provide clean water and better hygeiene to people without them . <p> Disputes with neighbours over the sharing of water from rivers that cross national boundaries also means tensions are rising as water shortages grow , said Michael Kugelman , a deputy director and South Asia expert at the Wilson Center , a policy think tank in Washington D.C. <p> " Countries that get along the least are forced to share and cooperate over water resources , and many major rivers originate in , or pass through , politically contested and tense areas , " he told the Thomson Reuters Foundation . <p> " So you have population growth , intensifying climate-change impacts , poor water management and geopolitical tensions . It 's a perfect storm for greater water insecurity , " he said . <p> India is entangled in water disputes with its eastern and western neighbours - Bangladesh and Pakistan - which accuse it of monopolising water flows moving downstream toward them . <p> To the north and northeast , however , India fears a loss of water to upstream China , which plans a series of dams over the Tsangpo river , called the Brahmaputra as it flows into eastern India . <h> " <p> So you have population growth , intensifying climate-change impacts , poor water management and geopolitical tensions . It 's a perfect storm for greater water insecurity . <h> Water losses <p> While India 's trans-boundary rivers are governed by treaties on how water should be shared , disputes are increasing as water shortages stoke tensions . <p> Apart from in Bhutan and Nepal , South Asia 's per capita water availability is already below the world average . The region could face widespread water scarcity - less than 1,000 cubic metres available per person - by 2025 , Kugelman said . <p> Almost 600 million people in India are at high risk of being unable to continue relying on surface water - including in the country 's northwest and south , where much of the country 's staple wheat and rice are grown , according to the World Resources Institute . <p> " Large parts of India have already been living with ' Day Zero ' for a while now , " said Mridula Ramesh , author of an upcoming book on climate change . <p> " Much of it is because of bad management . Most cities lose between a third and a fifth of their water from pilferage or leakage through antiquated pipes , and we do n't treat and reuse wastewater enough , " she said . <p> Bengaluru , Karachi and Kabul are among the 10 cities in the world that are " on the verge of an imminent water crisis " , according to a report last month by the Centre for Science and Environment ( CSE ) , a research and advocacy group based in New Delhi . <p> Bengaluru , once known as the " city of lakes " , now relies heavily on groundwater , which is not being naturally replenished and can not sustain the growing population , said Sushmita Sengupta at CSE . <p> " ' Day Zeros ' are inevitable unless cities push for judicious use of water - including rainwater harvesting and reuse of waste water , as well as more efficient irrigation , and regulation of tubewells , " she said . <h> Migration ? <p> India is one of the largest consumers of groundwater in the world , with worsening shortages attributed in part to subsidies that help farmers run electric irrigation pumps cheaply for longer than needed , and to a lack of limits on extracting water or digging wells . <p> Some states are taking steps to manage water better . Karnataka and Maharashtra require industries to use treated urban wastewater , with Gujarat and other states also planning similar measures . <p> But regulating water use is a politically sensitive issue - one few policymakers are keen to address , Kugelman said . That 's the case even as thousands of farmers migratw out of parched rural areas or commit suicide as their crops wilt , he said . . <p> Water scarcity is expected to force 50 to 70 million people in India , Bangladesh , Nepal and China from their homes by 2050 , according to research by the Strategic Foresight Group in Mumbai . <p> " With greater migration to the cities , there will be increased social disruptions and greater stress on water resources in urban areas , " Kugelman predicted . <p> This story was published with permission fromThomson Reuters Foundation , the charitable arm of Thomson Reuters , that covers humanitarian news , women 's rights , trafficking , property rights , climate change and resilience . @@24382425 <h> Time for a global financial makeover <h> Not enough has been done to leverage our financial systems in pursuit of the SDGs , as financing still flows to short-term investment projects , writes UN under-secretary-general for economic and social affairs , Liu Zhenmin . <p> The city of Hong Kong at night . Incentives toward long-term infrastructure projects like bridges , roads , and water and sewage systems should be created in order to make a major contribution to both development and stability , says Liu . Image : Christian Junker Photogrpahy , CC BY-SA 2.0 , via IFPRI Flicker <h> Share this story <p> In 2015 , United Nations member states came together and committed to achieving a comprehensive and universal set of 17 Sustainable Development Goals ( SDGs ) spanning all dimensions of economic and social development . <p> Investment will be indispensable to achieving the SDGs , which aim to eliminate poverty , end hunger , combat climate change , build resilient infrastructure , and promote inclusive and sustainable economic growth . Yet , three years on , we still have not done nearly enough to leverage our financial systems in pursuit of the SDGs . <p> The UN , in coordination with almost 60 agencies and international institutions , recently published an assessment of the world 's progress toward changing financing , policies , and regulations to achieve the SDGs . It finds that , despite positive momentum on sustainable investment , the goals will not be met unless we shift the entire financial system toward long-term investment horizons , and make sustainability a central concern . Without a long-term perspective , certain risks , especially those associated with climate change , will not be priced into private investment decisions . <p> Global financial flows are vast , yet the quality of investment matters . Currently , short-term investment patterns are driving capital-market and exchange-rate volatility , and significantly raising the costs and risks of sustainable investment , particularly for developing countries . If we create incentives to steer the flow of financing toward long-term infrastructure projects like bridges , roads , and water and sewage systems , we would be making a major contribution to both development and stability . <p> And those investment projects must be more environmentally and socially sustainable . Because today 's investments , particularly in energy systems , will lock in development paths for decades to come , more must be done to ensure that investments now , and in the future , do not undermine our efforts to address climate change . Moreover , as with all economic policies , gender equality needs to become a central consideration . <h> " <p> Despite positive momentum on sustainable investment , the goals will not be met unless we shift the entire financial system toward long-term investment horizons , and make sustainability a central concern . <p> Transforming finance will not be easy . Today 's capital markets are highly oriented toward short-termism , as evidenced by capital-flow volatility and the short holding period of stocks in some developed markets , which has fallen from an average of eight years in the 1960s to eight months today . And while long-term institutional investors hold around $80 trillion in assets , with about half of these representing long-term liabilities , nearly 75 per cent are held in liquid instruments , whereas just 3 per cent are in infrastructure . <p> The same tendency is prevalent in the real economy . In 2016 , S&amp;P 500 companies spent more than 100 per cent of their earnings on dividends and share buybacks , which boost stock prices in the short run , rather than raising long-term value through investment . A February 2017 McKinsey Global Institute survey found that 87 per cent of corporate executives and directors feel " pressured to demonstrate strong financial performance within two years or less , " while 65 per cent say that " short-term pressure has increased over the past five years . " Moreover , 55 per cent said they would delay investments in projects with positive returns in order to hit quarterly balance-sheet targets . <p> Shifting investors from short-termism toward long-term thinking is a prerequisite for achieving all of our economic , social , and environmental goals . But the private sector will not make this transition by itself . Policymakers must step in and provide leadership . Markets do not operate fairly and in the public interest without well-considered and well-enforced rules set by governments . Aside from public investment , this is one of the state 's most essential functions . <p> Specifically , transforming global finance will require changes in prudential regulations , capital requirements , investment-firm culture , and executive compensation , which will require new and more appropriate longer-term benchmarks . Reforms to accounting practices , especially for illiquid investments , will also be necessary , for example , to reduce the short-term bias introduced by mark-to-market accounting . And institutional investors must adopt a broader interpretation of fiduciary duty , which should focus on the long term and incorporate all factors that have a material impact on returns , be they financial , environmental , social , or governance-related . <p> With 12 years to go , it may seem like the world has plenty of time to make progress toward the SDGs . But the UN 's past experiences with goal-oriented initiatives show that it is important to take decisive action early on in the process . Making matters worse , escalating geopolitical and trade tensions threaten to set us back , rather than take us forward . Such disagreements must not stand in the way of reaching the SDGs and building a sustainable future . <p> Above all , that future needs to be financed . Though many public and private institutions at various levels of international finance have already started to change , the overall financial system has yet to experience the sort of transformation that is needed . We have all agreed on what we need to do ; now we must do it . <p> Liu Zhenmin is United Nations Under-Secretary-General for Economic and Social Affairs . <h> Thanks for reading to the end of this story ! <p> We would be grateful if you would consider joining as a member of The EB Circle . This helps to keep our stories and resources free for all , and it also supports independent journalism dedicated to sustainable development . It only costs as little as S$5 a month , and you would be helping to make a big difference .

@@24401564 <h> Scientists have put forward a new hypothesis for the extinction of Neanderthals <p> The Neanderthals appeared in Europe , Central Asia and the Middle East about 200 thousand years ago . <p> Japanese scientists from Keio University have made a new assumption about the cause of the extinction of the Neanderthals . Comparing models of brain of the Neanderthal and the first Homo sapiens , the researchers came to the conclusion that Neanderthals were less developed cerebellum . Because of this backlog , they are much inferior to our ancestors in the ability to coordinate movements and to explore the world . Unable to adapt to changing environmental conditions , they are extinct , I guess the Japanese experts . However , according to Russian experts , to put an end to this ancient history yet . <p> Their disappearance coincided roughly with the arrival in Europe of CRO-magnon ( early modern human ) 42 thousand years ago . These two species could survive together about 12 thousand years , after which the Neanderthals fell out of the evolutionary chain . <p> There are several hypotheses about the causes of the extinction of the Neanderthals . According to some researchers , the CRO-magnons could bring a number of diseases to which the Neanderthals had no immunity . Another hypothesis is that the Neanderthals could not adapt to abrupt climate change . The third version was that in the genes of the Neanderthals gradually accumulated harmful mutations that led to the disappearance of species.Scientists from Keio University have put forward another version . According to Japanese experts , the Neanderthals , unlike CRO-magnon were poorly developed cerebellum -- the part of the brain responsible for motor coordination and cognitive abilities . This , according to the study authors , was the cause of the extinction of the Neanderthals , who were unable to adapt to changing environmental conditions . <p> To such conclusions scientists have come , having compared the structure of the brain of the Neanderthals and the CRO-magnons . Japanese paleoanthropologists have studied the fragments of the skulls of two ancient predecessors of modern man and based on the data created casts of the brain boxes . They put a virtual model of the average brain of modern man , based on the results of MRI scans . In experiment have taken part more than thousand volunteers . <p> After studying with the help of computer analysis of the corresponding deformation of the virtual models , scientists were able to identify the differences areas of the brain in the CRO-magnon and Neanderthal . Among the early representatives of Homo sapiens , the volume of the cerebellum was significantly greater than that of the Neanderthals . " A large number of neurons in the region of the cerebellum at the CRO-magnons had a positive effect on intelligence , attention , ability to learn and remember information . This may have helped people better adapt to changing conditions , increasing their chances of survival , " -- said the study 's author Naomichi Ogihara . <h> Latest News <p> Trump will meet with Kim Jong Inom.The US President Donald trump said that a meeting with North Korean leader Kim Jong-UN must be held within three to four weeks . @@24402328 <h> Australia pledges half a billion to restore Great Barrier Reef <p> Scientists have said the reef suffered a ' catastrophic die-off ' of coral during an extended heatwave in 2016 <p> Australia pledged half a billion dollars to restore and protect the Great Barrier Reef Sunday in what it said would be a game-changer for the embattled natural wonder , but conservationists were not convinced . <p> The World Heritage-listed site , which attracts millions of tourists , is reeling from significant bouts of coral bleaching due to warming sea temperatures linked to climate change . <p> It is also under threat from the coral-eating crown-of-thorns starfish , which has proliferated due to pollution and agricultural runoff . <p> Prime Minister Malcolm Turnbull said more than Aus$500 million ( $400 million ) would go towards improving water quality , tackling predators , and expanding restoration efforts . <p> Turnbull said it was the " largest ever single investment -- to protect the reef , secure its viability and the 64,000 jobs that rely on the reef " . <p> " We want to ensure the reef 's future for the benefit of all Australians , particularly those whose livelihood depends on the reef , " he added . <p> The reef is a critical national asset , contributing Aus$6.4 billion a year to the Australian economy . <p> Canberra has previously committed more than Aus$2.0 billion to protect the site over the next decade , but has been criticised for backing a huge coal project by Indian mining giant Adani nearby . <p> With its heavy use of coal-fired power and relatively small population , Australia is considered one of the world 's worst per-capita greenhouse gas polluters . <p> Canberra insists it is taking strong action to address the global threat of climate change , having set an ambitious target to reduce emissions by 26 to 28 percent from 2005 levels by 2030 . <p> Turnbull said part of the money will be used to mitigate the impacts of climate change , but gave no details . <p> Conservationists said while the funding was " an important step " , the biggest threat to the reef was global warming and not enough was being done to combat it by embracing clean energy . <p> " Science is well aware of what 's killing the coral . It 's the excess heat from burning fossil fuels , " said Bill McKibben , founder of the global grassroots climate movement 350. org . <p> " To simultaneously promote the world 's biggest coal mine ( Adani ) while pretending to care about the world 's largest reef is an acrobatic feat only a cynical politician would attempt . " <p> Australian Conservation Foundation chief Kelly O'Shanassy agreed . <p> " Our elected representatives ca n't have it both ways , " she said . " Climate change is the number one threat to the Great Barrier Reef and only concerted action to cut pollution will fully protect it . " <p> - Resilient reef - <p> The bulk of the new funding -- just over Aus$200 million -- was earmarked to improve water quality by changing farming practices and adopting new technologies and land management . <p> " The money will go towards improving water quality , working with farmers to prevent sediment , nitrogen and pesticide runoff into the reef , " said Environment Minister Josh Frydenberg . <p> " It will ensure that we tackle the crown-of-thorns ... and use the best available science to ensure our coral is resilient to heat and light stress . " <p> He said the government would work with traditional Aboriginal owners , the tourist industry , farmers and scientists , to save the reef , calling the commitment " a game-changer " . <p> Earlier this month , scientists said the site suffered a " catastrophic die-off " of coral during an extended heatwave in 2016 , threatening a broader range of reef life than previously feared . <p> A study in the journal Nature said some 30 percent of the reef 's coral perished , the first of an unprecedented two successive years of coral bleaching along the 2,300-kilometre ( 1,400-mile ) reef . 
@@24413724 <h> Researchers discover world 's oldest spider in Australia <p> A group of Australian scientists have claimed that they have discovered the oldest trapdoor spider in the world but the spider , which was 43-year-old , died recently during a long-term population study in Western Australia 's Central Wheatbelt . <p> The lead author of this study Leanda Mason , who is also a PhD student from the School of Molecular and Life Sciences at Curtin University , has claimed that it was the oldest spider recorded ever . The particular spider , which is recognized as the Giaus Villosus trapdoor matriarch , helped the research team to understand the behaviour of trapdoor spiders and how the creatures interact with each other . <p> The death of the trapdoor matriarch has snatched the record of world 's oldest spider from its previous rival , a 28-year-old Mexican tarantula . <p> The study , which was published in the Pacific Conservation Biology Journal , also showed that how such long-term studies are important to understand the growth of these species in the Australian environment . <p> As per the Curtin University reports , Mason said that this research project was first initiated in 1974 , by Barbara York Main , who monitored the long-term spider population for over 42 years in Western Australia . <p> " Through Barbara 's detailed research , we were able to determine that the extended lifespan of the trapdoor spider is due to their life-history traits , including how they live in uncleared , native bush land , their sedentary nature and low metabolisms , " she further added . <p> Mason and other researchers are continuing their research on the life and habitat of world 's oldest spider . The co-author of this study Grant Wardell-Johnson from the Centre for Mine Site Restoration ( CMSR ) , has explained that through this ongoing research process they hope to identify how spider 's behavioural characteristics help them to survive . <p> He said , " These spiders exemplify an approach to life in ancient landscapes , and through our ongoing research we will be able to determine how the future stresses of climate change and deforestation will potentially impact the species . " <p> Facts about Trapdoor Spiders : <p> It can be around 2.5 centimetres long on average . <p> It lives underground for most of its life . In countries like Japan , Africa , South America and North America and many other warm places they can be found . <p> They do not have web-like other spiders do . It has a trapdoor on top of a burrow , which is around 30 centimetres deep and 5 centimetres across . <p> Their diet includes all types of insects even frogs , baby birds , baby snakes , mice and small fish . <p> Female trapdoor spiders lay eggs several months after mating and protect them inside the burrow . <p> The bite of the trapdoor spider is of low risk ( non-toxic ) to humans . <p> They are often kept as exotic pets but these spiders are very aggressive and should only be kept by experienced people . 
@@24416593 <h> Chance to highlight plight of Arctic animals <p> It was with great consolation that I read that the Singapore Zoo has set up a memorial wall as part of its tribute to Singapore 's beloved polar bear ( Zoo staff pay tribute to Inuka ; April 27 ) . <p> Even though Inuka was a polar bear born and bred in tropical Singapore , and had never set foot in the Arctic , he can still be an ambassador for his fellow polar bears in the wild . <p> The public has expressed worries over the welfare of wild animals , like polar bears , which are kept in enclosed areas like zoos . <p> However , doing so gives scientists access to a small gene pool as they work to support the propagation of a species , should the wild population of that animal dip too close to extinction , just like what China has been doing with the giant panda . <p> And indeed , many studies have indicated that polar bears face an unprecedented reduction of their population and even a high risk of extinction in the wild due to the premature melting of sea ice , which is , in turn , caused by climate change ( First polar bear born in Britain in 25 years ; ST Online , March 17 ) . <p> It is well-known that polar bears depend on sea ice to effectively hunt for seals , which are their most important source of food . <p> But documentaries have filmed polar bears scavenging or resorting to eating seaweed or seagulls ' eggs in apparent desperation as the sea ice shrinks . <p> As we grieve Inuka 's passing , we must also bear in mind the bleak plight faced by polar bears and other animals whose survival has been upset by our unsustainable burning of fossil fuels , agricultural practices and way of life . <p> Perhaps the zoo should also set up exhibitions , in addition to the memorial wall , to inform people of exactly what damage climate change is causing to the Arctic region , and to call for action to mitigate it . <p> There should also be a reminder that everyone must do his part to save the environment , be it recycling , switching to public transport or just by using less electricity . <p> This message could be reiterated on subsequent anniversaries of Inuka 's death to remind the public that they are responsible for the upkeep of the planet and its biodiversity . <p> If we succeed in getting this message across to the public , Inuka would not have died in vain . <p> Lee Kay Yan ( Miss ) <p> A version of this article appeared in the print edition of The Straits Times on April 30 , 2018 , with the headline ' Chance to highlight plight of Arctic animals ' . @@24417800 <h> World 's oldest known spider dies in Australia -- of wasp sting <p> Named Number 16 , the spider helped scientists to unlock important information about the behaviour of an arachnid that can be found across Australia , including in domestic gardens <p> The world 's oldest known spider has died at the ripe old age of 43 after being monitored for years during a long-term population study in Australia , researchers said Monday . <p> The trapdoor matriarch comfortably outlived the previous record holder , a 28-year-old tarantula found in Mexico , according to a study published in the Pacific Conservation Biology Journal . <p> The spider did not die of old age but was killed by a wasp sting , researchers said . <p> Named Number 16 , the spider helped scientists to unlock important information about the behaviour of an arachnid that can be found across Australia , including in domestic gardens . <p> " To our knowledge this is the oldest spider ever recorded , and her significant life has allowed us to further investigate the trapdoor spider 's behaviour and population dynamics , " said lead author Leanda Mason from Curtin University . <p> A research project to study trapdoor spiders in the Central Wheatbelt region of Western Australia was first launched in 1974 by Barbara York Main , during which Number 16 was found and monitored . <p> " Through Barbara 's detailed research , we were able to determine that the extensive life span of the trapdoor spider is due to their life-history traits , including how they live in uncleared , native bushland , their sedentary nature and low metabolisms , " said Mason . <p> Number 16 was monitored in the wild . Female trapdoor spiders stay in and around the same burrow virtually all their lives , so researchers marked her burrow and went back to check on it regularly . <p> The study also gave a better understanding of how the future stresses of climate change and deforestation could impact the species . <p> Trapdoor spiders traditionally have a life span between five to 20 years . While females stay in or near their burrows , males leave once mature and go in search of a mate . <p> They are not a major threat to humans , although a bite can cause pain and swelling .

@@24429033 <h> In the article , the writer says it is time for countries to think beyond borders and work in the interests of a wider community . <p> Laurence Brahm <p> BEIJING ( CHINA DAILY/ASIA NEWS NETWORK ) - The dragon and the tiger are two ancient Asian symbols that express dynamism and strength in both China and India . <p> The ancient Chinese science of geomancy or feng shui always necessitated having dragon and tiger elements in balance and synergy to assure both prosperity and peace . <p> According to Hindu tradition , the god Vishnu assures maintenance , sustainability and prosperity of all existence , by floating dreamlike upon a bed of entangled Naga or serpent dragons . <p> Such symbolic innuendo will not be lost among the leaders of two nations that share over five thousand years of unbroken history , cultural and economic exchange among themselves , having both served as main sources of our world 's heritage and culture . <p> As China 's President Xi jinping and Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi meet in Wuhan on April 28-29 , 2018 , it potentially heralds a re-start of the ancient Silk Road . <p> For millennia the Silk Road network of trade and commerce represented the economic order of our planet . <p> From the historical perspective of both China and India , the two-hundred plus years of colonial , post-colonial and neo-liberal eras that sidelined these traditional exchange routes , marked only a very brief disruption in the overarching sequence of their shared history . <p> As President Xi and Prime Minister Modi shook hands in Wuhan last weekend , it could represent a historic moment . Yes , an ancient yet new strategic alliance that could prove a global game changer . <p> These two powers through policy coordination could implode a regional mega-trend of smart infrastructure integration , enhanced through technology , telecommunications , and digital finance . <p> Today China stands as the largest single global investor in artificial intelligence and quantum communications . <p> India is the world leader in software outsourcing and IT consulting . <p> Both nations are the largest innovators and users of digital financing networks and cell phone banking systems , pioneering financial empowerment into the most isolated villages where their low-cost smart phone penetration is already strong . <p> The future of innovation is no longer in Silicon Valley , but rather in the arc spreading from China 's Great Bay ( Shenzhen-Hong Kong ) to India 's Bangalore . <p> The question now , is how to better integrate it . <p> China 's Belt and Road offers a blueprint for integration . <p> So does India 's Act East policy . <p> For consecutive years , the Himalayan Consensus Summit held annually in Kathmandu has called for both policies to become integral and synergistic rather than viewed as separate and isolated from each other . <p> To say that India is not a participant in China 's Belt and Road Initiative ( BRI ) is to ignore the fact that alongside China , India is the second largest investor in both the Asian Infrastructure Investment Bank and the ( BRICS ) New Development Bank . <p> Both serve as the leading financial institutions for the BRI . <p> In the area of infrastructure and communications , both nations will only gain from coordinating and synergising their respective development policies . <p> Such new infrastructure will need to be smart , green and blue . <p> Certainly China and India are now leaders in scaling renewable energy innovations as viable commercial solutions . <p> Environment and climate change are the most critical issues requiring information and policy coordination . <p> Both nations are the treasurers of our planet 's vital resource , the Himalayan glaciers . <p> River systems from shared glaciers are the main water source for one quarter of the world 's population stretching across Central , South and Southeast Asia . <p> Thousands of trans-boundary river systems pose challenges of both water supply and sanitation . <p> As the two leading regional powers , both China and India must act together in averting imminent water crisis that could pose unprecedented costs of water , food and health security along with natural disasters , migration , and regional instability . <p> For this very reason , Professor Mahendra Lama , one of India 's Eminent Persons and member of Himalayan Consensus board , proposed at the 2016 Himalayan Consensus Summit that an alternative people-to-people and think tank mechanism should be established to mitigate crisis and avert conflict by addressing root causes rather than effects . <p> Nirupama Rao , former Indian Foreign Secretary and member of Himalayan Consensus board , called for a Himalayan Charter for the establishment of a Himalayan Council where regional governments can convene on climate concerns as proposed by ICIMOD Director General , David Molden , also Himalayan Consensus board member . <p> In this time of global volatility , when western leaders and policy makers are lost amidst political myopia , disregard of both long-term climate and human dignity in favour of short-term greed , our planet is in search of a new direction to avert climate crisis and war . <p> That voice of sanity may very well come from two ancient nations who have historically offered the world some of its deepest philosophical foundations . Europe may need to think about rebalance . Africa , whose largest inbound investors are China and India , can see the equation clearly . <p> South America may be thinking along the same lines . <p> This is a time when no nation can put itself first , and everyone must coordinate and work together for the sustainability if not existence of our planet . <p> It is time to realise a " community of shared future for mankind " as a vision to shared among us all . <p> Maybe now is time to " act East " and not look west . <p> The writer is the Founding Director of Himalayan Consensus , an environmental think tank , and International Research Fellow with the China Center for Globalisation . @@24430258 <h> Share this story <p> Innovators and entrepreneurs are working to speed up the transition towards clean and renewable energy sources , but many of the innovative clean energy projects have trouble raising the finance to turn their ideas into reality . <p> This was the case for ATEC , a social enterprise in Cambodia which has developed appropriate technology to address the health and environmental issues associated with cooking with wood and charcoal . <p> ATEC produces a small-scale biodigester system for rural Cambodian households . The basis of the system is a biodigester container which can be filled with a combination of organic waste -- such as leftovers from the kitchen and animal manure -- and water . When the waste decomposes it produces methane gas , which can be used for cooking . As well as providing a free and sustainable energy source , the process leaves waste that can also be used as a natural fertilizer for crops . The use of the ATEC biodigester system can save the average Cambodian family around $254 per year . <p> PFAN 's goal is to bridge the gap between investors and entrepreneurs . It identifies promising clean energy projects at an early stage and provides no-cost coaching and investment facilitation -- that includes mentoring for the development of a business plan , investment pitches and a growth strategy . When in place , these measures make it easier for investors to understand a project 's potential , increasing the odds that they will make an investment . <p> ATEC was one of the twelve companies to pitch their product to a group of investors at the 7th Asia Forum for Clean Energy Financing 2017 in Singapore . The event , organised by PFAN , brought together more than 80 investors from across the globe . The company came out as second runner-up and the exposure it received led it to finding the funding necessary to scale up its operations . ATEC is now present in 13 provinces in Cambodia and is looking to expand into neighbouring countries such as Myanmar and Indonesia . <p> So far around 487 clean energy initiatives have been inducted into PFAN project development pipeline . The initiatives are developing appropriate technologies to provide biogas , biomass , waste-to-energy , clean transport , wind , solar , small hydro , rural electrification and energy efficiency solutions . Eighty-seven of them have achieved financial closure with over US$ 1.24bn of investment raised . Combined , these enterprises have the potential to mitigate over 3.3 million tons of CO2e emissions per year and provide over 802 MW of clean energy capacity . <p> Access to sustainable and clean energy sources is essential to the goals set by the Agenda 2030 and the Paris Agreement on climate change . It also contributes to a higher standing of living , and better health and education , and is crucial for the productive activities that can raise people out of poverty . <p> One of the main barriers to implementing renewable energy solutions is the lack of funding , especially in developing countries . In a world where one billion people still lack access to electricity and three billion do n't have access to clean cooking , a lot more investment is needed . Initiatives like PFAN work to fill this gap and provide people with access to affordable , clean energy . <h> Thanks for reading to the end of this story ! <p> We would be grateful if you would consider joining as a member of The EB Circle . This helps to keep our stories and resources free for all , and it also supports independent journalism dedicated to sustainable development . It only costs as little as S$5 a month , and you would be helping to make a big difference . 
@@24430670 <h> Scientists have found that can change the Earth 's climate <p> American climate scientists found that slowing the evaporation of water from the leaves of tropical plants in response to increasing concentrations of carbon dioxide can lead to asymmetric changes in the map precipitation on the planet . <p> So , in the tropical forests of the Amazon can become much drier , and in Indonesia the precipitations , on the contrary , will grow . <p> Increasing the concentration of greenhouse gases in the atmosphere over the last 150 years has resulted in significant changes in Earth 's climate : increased temperature and ocean surface , frequent droughts , changing rainfall and frequency of dangerous weather phenomena . <p> Usually , to assess the effect of greenhouse gases such as carbon dioxide , scientists use computer simulations , which take into account only the purely climatic and thermodynamic phenomena : change of the direction of air and water flows , the redistribution of concentrations of certain substances , or changing the maps of temperature and humidity . <p> American climate scientists under the leadership of Gabriel Cooperman ( Gabriel Kooperman ) University of California , Irvine showed that another important factor that may affect the amount of precipitation in tropical zones may be a physiological response of tropical forests to increase carbon dioxide concentration . The natural physiological response of plants to increasing CO2 is a decrease in the degree of opening of the stomata on the leaves , the size of which varies from 10 to 60 microns . This leads to the fact that the transfer of all gases through the stomata is slowing down : slowing down and capture of carbon dioxide from the atmosphere , and evaporation of water by transpiration . <p> And slowing of transpiration , in turn , affects the water content in the soil , which then becomes one of the causes of the warming of the atmosphere and changes in precipitation . Analysis of the annual rings of trees shows that is particularly sensitive to this effect of tropical forests , and that they most strongly slowed down transpiration over the past half century , when the increase in carbon dioxide has become the most noticeable . <p> To show how such a mechanism ( together with other climatic effects ) can affect climate change , scientists conducted a computer simulation in which the calculated total " natural " forest effect on the change in rainfall on the planet by the end of the XXI century , and were separately modeled the three tropical zones in South America , Asia and Africa . <p> It turned out that the slowdown of transpiration may lead to asymmetric changes in the quantity of rain in different tropical zones of the Earth . So , in the Amazon basin estimated average precipitation by the end of the XXI century may fall to 1 millimeter a day , and in Indonesia -- on the contrary , grow by the same amount . Interestingly , this effect is primarily determined by local climatic processes in the same region , and global processes make only small changes ( especially in Africa ) , but are not indicative of the asymmetry of this phenomenon . <p> Scientists explain this asymmetry is the fact that Amazonia is much more sensitive to changes in the cycle of moisture transport between ground and atmosphere , and that transpiration plays in this cycle is one of the defining roles . In South-East Asia as slowing the evaporation process of moisture from the leaf surface completely kompensiruet another effect : due to the increasing temperature difference between land and sea , there is a strengthening of the winds that blow towards the land and bring with it a large number of clouds , which leads to increased rainfall . <p> The study authors noted that their study clearly shows that a change in the physiology of plants can be one of the important factors in the assessment of climate change on the planet . However , further studies of processes of redistribution of carbon dioxide and water in the atmosphere due to plant responses to increasing greenhouse gases is a need for more detailed studies . <h> Latest News <p> The app has been blocked by court order.The Iranian court ruled Monday to ban the use Telegram messenger on the territory of the whole country . This was reported by local state TV channel . @@24431913 <h> Lake Victoria biodiversity being ' decimated ' : conservationists <p> Lake Victoria is a major migratory destination for several large fauna species in East Africa <p> Three quarters of freshwater species endemic to East Africa 's Lake Victoria basin face the threat of extinction , conservationists said Monday , warning the biodiversity there was being " decimated " . <p> A fresh report backed by the International Union for Conservation of Nature ( IUCN ) assessed the extinction risk of 651 freshwater species like fish , molluscs , dragonflies , crabs and aquatic plants native to Africa 's largest lake . <p> It found that a full 20 percent of these species were threatened with extinction . <p> The picture was however far darker when looking only at the freshwater species endemic to the area -- 204 of those assessed , according to the report titled " Freshwater biodiversity in the Lake Victoria Basin " . <p> " Three-quarters ( 76 percent ) of these endemics are at risk of extinction , " IUCN warned in a statement . <p> In its report , the Switzerland-based organisation pointed out that freshwater species are important sources of food , medicine and construction material for the millions of people living in the area surrounding the lake . <p> The lake , which stretches into Kenya , Tanzania and Uganda and whose catchment also touches Burundi and Rwanda , is known for its high-level of unique biodiversity . <p> " The Lake Victoria Basin is incredibly rich in unique species found nowhere else on Earth , yet its biodiversity is being decimated , " said Will Darwall , a co-author of the report who heads IUCN 's freshwater biodiversity unit . <p> - ' Disastrous ' - <p> " The effects on communities that depend on the lake for their livelihoods could be disastrous , " he warned in the statement . <p> The report pointed for instance to the African Lungfish , a long eel-like fish , which it said has seen its numbers dwindle due largely to overfishing , poor fishing practices and environmental degradation as wetlands are converted to agricultural land . <p> Industrial and agricultural pollution , over-harvesting and land clearance are among the main threats to biodiversity in the region , the report said . <p> It also pointed to a significant impact of climate change , noting that freshwater fish have " high sensitivity ( and ) seemingly poor adaptive capacity " to climatic shifts . <p> And it stressed the threat from invasive species to the native biodiversity in the basin , highlighting the South American purple-flowered Water Hyacinth , which was accidentally introduced to the lake in the 1980s . <p> At its peak , the plant covered nearly 10 percent of the lake surface in dense floating mats , which served to reduce the oxygen and nutrients available , impacting the native biodiversity , IUCN said . <p> " The risk of extinction for freshwater biodiversity in the region is increasing , " co-author of the report Catherine Sayer said in the statement . <p> Hundreds of the species present in the lake had yet to be charted , meaning " the number of species at high risk of extinction may be even greater then we currently realise , " she added . 
@@24433070 <p> French President Emmanuel Macron holds a town hall meeting with U.S. students at George Washington University in Washington , April 25 , 2018 . REUTERS/Brian Snyder <p> PARIS ( Reuters ) - French President Emmanuel Macron heads to Australia on Tuesday in hopes of cementing defence ties , pursuing his agenda to boost France 's profile beyond Europe in a region where it maintains a string of island territories . <p> Presidential aides said the military partnership with Australia was becoming crucial at a time of tension in the South China Sea between rival emerging nations , a region that is also a focus of global trade . <p> France cites its small island territories in the Indian and Pacific oceans in its claims as an Asia-Pacific regional power . <p> Macron will be travelling with a delegation of small- and mid-sized military and naval contractors to build on a $38 billion contract France won in 2016 to supply submarines to Australia . <p> " The idea is to transform this partnership , which made a very impressive jump forward with the submarine contract , into a catalyst to boost trade , " an official from Macron 's office said . <p> Australia selected France 's DCNS - now called Naval Group - in 2016 as its preferred bidder to build its fleet of 12 submarines , ahead of other offers from Japan and Germany - one of the world 's most lucrative defence contracts . <p> Macron 's trip will be the first strictly bilateral visit by a French president to Australia and comes soon after a three-day visit to Washington , where he flaunted his friendship with President Donald Trump while challenging many of his policies , urging the United States to engage more with the world , step up the fight against climate change and stay in the Iran nuclear pact for now . <p> Macron 's time in Australia will also provide plenty of the photo opportunities at iconic sites of which the energetic 40-year-old French leader is fond . <p> After landing in Sydney , Macron will head to dinner with Prime Minister Malcolm Turnbull at the famed Opera House in the city 's harbour . He will also talk up French gastronomy at a French bistro , visit an art gallery and meet Aboriginal artists . <p> In a more sensitive part of his trip , Macron will then head off to the French island of New Caledonia , which will vote in November on whether to break with France and become independent . <p> French officials said Macron would refrain from giving voting recommendations . <p> But he will seek to strike a balance between commemorating the past on the 30th anniversary of the " Ouvea cave massacre " , in which 19 indigenous separatists and two French soldiers were killed , and talking about the future of the nickel-rich island . 
@@24434338 <h> PUB eyeing two more reservoirs to harness solar power <p> The floating solar panel test bed at Tengeh Reservoir is the world 's largest . PUB is looking at adding solar systems to Bedok and Lower Seletar reservoirs to power its energy-intensive water treatment processes in a greener way.ST FILE PHOTO <h> Tender called for studies on Bedok and Lower Seletar to facilitate use of such systems <p> With little space to accommodate sprawling solar fields , Singapore is looking into installing solar panels on its water spaces . <p> Engineering studies for solar systems are being carried out at Tengeh and Upper Peirce reservoirs , and now national water agency PUB is looking at adding them to two more reservoirs here to power its energy-intensive water treatment processes in a greener way . <p> Yesterday , it called a tender for engineering studies to be carried out at Bedok and Lower Seletar reservoirs to facilitate the deployment of floating solar photovoltaic systems there . <p> A solar system will also be installed on the roof of Bedok Waterworks by this year , PUB said . <p> Energy is required throughout the water loop - from the pumping of raw water from reservoirs to waterworks , to the production of drinking water , treatment of wastewater , and production of Newater and desalinated water . <p> " There is potential to replace some of the grid energy needed by its operations with solar energy generated by solar panels installed on the roof spaces of existing installations and on the surfaces of the reservoirs , " the agency said . <p> More than 95 per cent of Singapore 's grid energy comes from the burning of natural gas . Though considered the cleanest form of fossil fuel , its combustion still contributes to the production of greenhouse gases . <p> Renewable energy from the sun , however , would reduce Singapore 's reliance on fossil fuels . Its use is also in line with its target to reduce greenhouse gas emissions . <p> While most solar panels are deployed on land or rooftops , the use of water bodies is especially beneficial for land-scarce countries like Singapore . Solar systems would supply energy for the pumping of raw water from Bedok Reservoir to Bedok Waterworks for treatment , and the pumping of treated water into the water supply network for households . <p> Energy generated by the floating solar system at Lower Seletar Reservoir would go to pumping raw water for treatment , and for the transfer of raw water between reservoirs for operational purposes . <p> Having solar systems at both reservoirs would reduce PUB 's carbon footprint by an estimated 1.3 kilotonnes of carbon dioxide annually , equivalent to taking about 270 cars off the road every year . <p> PUB also assured the public that the panels would be installed away from current water activity zones at both reservoirs . <p> The system at Lower Seletar Reservoir is slated to occupy about 1ha of water surface space , and the one in Bedok about 1.5ha . Each system will take up less than 2 per cent of total surface area at each reservoir . <p> PUB chief executive Ng Joo Hee said the agency is committed to making its operations more environmentally sustainable , adding : " The more renewables PUB can generate and use , the smaller our carbon footprint , and the greater our contribution to Singapore 's climate change mitigation effort . " <p> Dr Thomas Reindl , deputy chief executive of the Solar Energy Research Institute of Singapore ( Seris ) , said that while solar photovoltaic systems in Singapore tend to be small in nature , the collective carbon reduction through all the installed solar power in Singapore is significant . <p> He added that floating solar systems are part of a growing trend globally - one that Singapore is leading in terms of research and development . <p> The Tengeh test bed , for example , which Seris operates with PUB and the Economic Development Board , is the world 's largest . <p> Moreover , research into floating solar systems on reservoirs is a good first step in tapping the larger marine environment . <p> Dr Reindl told The Straits Times : " This has great potential for Singapore , due to the sheltered nature of the surrounding sea with no big waves . " <h> The Straits Times <p> We have been experiencing some problems with subscriber log-ins and apologise for the inconvenience caused . Until we resolve the issues , subscribers need not log in to access ST Digital articles . But a log-in is still required for our PDFs. 
@@24434630 <h> Market voices on : <p> Named Number 16 , the spider helped scientists to unlock important information about the behaviour of an arachnid that can be found across Australia , including in domestic gardens . <p> " To our knowledge this is the oldest spider ever recorded , and her significant life has allowed us to further investigate the trapdoor spider 's behaviour and population dynamics , " said lead author Leanda Mason from Curtin University . <p> A research project to study trapdoor spiders in the Central Wheatbelt region of Western Australia was first launched in 1974 by Barbara York Main , during which Number 16 was found and monitored . " Through Barbara 's detailed research , we were able to determine that the extensive life span of the trapdoor spider is due to their life-history traits , including how they live in uncleared , native bushland , their sedentary nature and low metabolisms , " said Ms Mason . <p> Number 16 was monitored in the wild . Female trapdoor spiders stay in and around the same burrow virtually all their lives , so researchers marked her burrow and went back to check on it regularly . <p> The study also gave a better understanding of how the future stresses of climate change and deforestation could impact the species . <p> Trapdoor spiders traditionally have a life span between five to 20 years . They are not a major threat to humans , although a bite can cause pain and swelling . AFP
